Topological field theory plus local Lorentz symmetry is gravity
Maïté Dupuis 1 , Florian Girelli 2 , Oleksandra Hrytseniak 1,3 , Wolfgang Wieland 4

Abstract
Four-dimensional gravity admits many equivalent formulations—metric, Einstein–Cartan, teleparallel, McDowell–Mansouri, among others—each offering distinct advantages, particularly, in view of quantization. We propose a new formulation based on Weyl spinor–valued 1-forms, ultimately encoding the frame-field data. Starting from a topological field theory with a global SL ⁡ ( 2 , ℂ ) \mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C}) symmetry, we show that promoting this symmetry to a local gauge symmetry leads to the emergence of gravity. We analyze the covariant phase space of this theory, its symmetries and charge structure and explore the role of admissible corner terms together with their impact on boundary charges and their algebra. We study several extensions of this framework, including the incorporation of a cosmological constant and a novel G → 0 G\rightarrow 0 scaling limit obtained from this model. The presence of the frame field already at the topological level allows point particles to be coupled uniformly in both the topological and gravitational theories. We perform a detailed Hamiltonian analysis of the theory and clarify the implementation of the reality conditions. We argue that this formulation provides structural features that make it particularly well suited for both discretization and quantization.



Introduction

In his book on gravitation
49
, Steven Weinberg says that the only good reason for gravitational variables to appear in the matter Lagrangian is to guarantee local Lorentz invariance. This gauge principle reflects the freedom of choosing independent inertial frames in different spacetime regions. It is realized by upgrading partial derivatives to Lorentz-covariant derivatives. Here, we show that this principle not only applies to the matter sector, but can be extended to the gravitational action itself.

We start from a topological field theory in four spacetime dimensions that admits a global Lorentz symmetry. We then promote this global Lorentz symmetry to a local gauge symmetry. This procedure breaks certain topological gauge symmetries—the so-called shift symmetry of BF-theory
6
,
28
,
33
,
56
,
13
. The breaking of the topological symmetries makes otherwise unphysical gauge directions become physical, thus unlocking propagating degrees of freedom. The resulting field theory describes self-dual gravity, which has two complex physical degrees of freedom per point. Upon imposing reality conditions, we recover the dynamics of general relativity. In this sense, gravity emerges from a gauge symmetry breaking of a topological field theory. A similar mechanism appears in Plebański’s formulation of gravity as a constrained topological field theory
37
. The main conceptual difference is that in our case we break topological symmetries by adding local gauge symmetries.

As a result, we rediscover Robinson’s formulation of complexified gravity in terms of Weyl spinor-valued
p
-forms
39
,
40
. A similar formulation in terms of Dirac spinor–valued fields first appeared in
35
. A version in terms of Weyl spinor-valued
1
-forms was first introduced in Tung and Jacobson
45
, in which the expressions for quasi-local energy take a particularly simple form. Robinson defined a first-order version of Tung and Jacobson’s action
and discussed reality conditions to recover gravity
39
,
40
.

Indeed, self-dual gravity can be understood as a complexification of general relativity. Thus, it has twice as many variables. To restore general relativity in terms of real-valued fields, one re-introduces an anti-self-dual part to the action. The two sectors are matched by reality conditions that select a real section of the complex phase space. Upon imposing constraints and quotienting out gauge symmetries, we are left with two physical degrees of freedom, representing the two modes of gravitational radiation.

Our paper provides a new perspective on some well-established results on gravitational symmetries and boundary charges. We study the charge algebra in the topological and gravitational theories using the covariant phase space formalism. In particular, we show how natural corner term ambiguities affect the values of the boundary charges and their algebra. Furthermore, we show that the reality conditions between the self-dual and anti-self-dual
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
sectors induce reality conditions on the corner charges.

Additionally, we consider several new developments of Robinson’s theory. First, we discuss how to add a cosmological constant term to the action. Second, by re-establishing Newton’s constant
G
in the action, we show that there is a new limit
G\rightarrow 0
of gravity, which can be unlocked naturally in the Weyl spinor variables. This limit can be considered at the level of the topological model as well, modifying the symmetry structure from the identity 2-group to a skeletal 2-group
6
,
13
. Finally, we discuss the coupling of point-like particle defects. Such particle defects can be coupled naturally to the topological theory. The symmetry-breaking mechanism that turns the topological field theory to (self-dual) gravity can be then also applied to the coupled system. The resulting coupled system consists of point particles coupled to (self-dual) gravity. Such a procedure cannot be done in the Plebański formulation, but it is similar to what is done in the McDowell-Mansouri approach
21
. The key feature is the presence of the (co)frame-field data already at the topological level.

In the second half of the paper, we perform a detailed Hamiltonian analysis of the spinorial formulation of the gravitational action. The Poisson commutation relations among the fundamental first-class and second-class constraints take a very simple form: the Dirac matrix of constraints depends on the fundamental fields at most quadratically. We also recover the Hamiltonian originally introduced by Tung and Jacobson
45
, given by a sum of a bulk constraint and boundary charges. Going on shell, we obtain the familiar result that the gravitational Hamiltonian is a codimension-2 surface integral. We study the imposition of the reality conditions relative to a dynamical spin basis, which is built from the dynamical fields in a
3+1
split of the theory. The construction allows to impose the reality conditions without breaking the internal
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
gauge symmetry.

Paper organization

The paper is organized as follows. In
Section
1
, we introduce the Weyl spinor topological action and perform its symmetry analysis, from a covariant phase space perspective. We highlight, in particular, the global
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
symmetry, as well as a hidden
\overline{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})}
local symmetry. In
Section
2
, we show how by including the Gauss constraint and the associated Lagrange multiplier, the connection, one can recover self-dual gravity. We perform the symmetry analysis of the gravitational action expressed in terms of Weyl spinor-valued 1-forms and 2-forms and discuss how the cosmological constant is introduced in this context. In
Section
3
, we discuss some of the corner terms we can add to change the charge structure of the gravity theory. We highlight how the reality conditions are realized in terms of the charges.
In
Section
4
, we discuss how this formalism allows to perform the limit
G\to 0
in a new way. We review how the symmetries are affected by such a limit, both in the topological and gravitational cases. In
Section
5
, we perform the Hamiltonian analysis of the theory to identify the first- and second-class constraints and perform the counting of degrees of freedom. We also discuss how reality conditions are stable under evolution. In
Section
6
, we show that the (spinless) particle can be introduced in the same way in the topological and gravitational theories. Finally, in the
Outlook
, we highlight the different strengths of the spinorial formulation of gravity in view of discretization and quantization.

Notation

Following standard notation,
36
, we use capital Latin letters to denote
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
indices
A,B,...
, which take values
0,1
, and are raised and lowered by the internal skew-symmetric
\epsilon
-tensors according to the pattern
\xi^{A}=\epsilon^{AB}\xi_{B}
,
\xi_{A}=\xi^{B}\epsilon_{BA}
. Primed indices
A^{\prime},B^{\prime},...
, correspond to the complex conjugate representation,
\overline{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})}
, upon imposing reality conditions.

In the following,
M
is a four-dimensional manifold with a boundary
\partial M
. We denote with
\Omega^{p}(M)
the space of
p
-forms on
M
. Let the boundary
\partial M
be piecewise-smooth and composed of smooth three-dimensional hypersurfaces
\{\Sigma_{i}\}
so that
\partial M=\bigcup_{i}\Sigma_{i}
. Each smooth boundary component
\Sigma_{i}
is itself bounded by a co-dimension-2 submanifold
C_{i}=\partial\Sigma_{i}
, which will be called a
corner
.

In what follows,
\psi_{A},\phi_{A}\in\Omega^{1}(M)\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
are Weyl spinor-valued 1-forms, and
\pi_{A},\rho_{A}\in\Omega^{2}(M)\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
Weyl spinor-valued 2-forms. Spacetime indices are denoted by
\mu,\nu,\rho,\dots
and
a,b,c,\dots
denote three-dimensional indices on a hypersurface. Depending on the context, we will often suppress the spinor or spacetime indices, thereby denoting e.g. the self-dual connection 1-form as
A
,
\mathchoice{A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}}{A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}}{A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}}{A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.383pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.96252pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.83037pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}}
or
\mathchoice{A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[10.72064pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.72064pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.55261pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.68042pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{Ba}}}}{A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[10.72064pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.72064pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.55261pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.68042pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{Ba}}}}{A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[10.72064pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.72064pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.55261pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.68042pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{Ba}}}}{A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[10.72064pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.72064pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.55261pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.68042pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.01392pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.675pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{Ba}}}}
. The infinite-dimensional space of all these fields
\phi,\psi,A,\pi,\rho
on
M
is denoted as
{\cal F}
. In what follows,
\bbvar{d}
denotes the field-space exterior derivative, while
\bbvar{I}_{\delta}\Phi
denotes the interior product of a field-space differential form
\Phi
with a vector field
\delta\in T\mathcal{F}
over the field space. By abuse of notation, we also denote by
\bbvar{I}_{\xi}\Phi
the contraction of the field space
p
-form with a symmetry generator
\delta_{\xi}\in T\mathcal{F}
, which generates a specific gauge transformation parametrized by a gauge parameter
\xi
.

We will note
\Omega^{p,q}(M,{\cal F})
the space of spacetime
p
-forms and
q
-forms in field space. For example,
\psi_{A}\wedge\bbvar{d}\phi^{A}\in\Omega^{2,1}(M,{\cal F})
.
In what follows, the wedge product
\wedge
denotes the wedge product on spacetime, as well as on field space where necessary.

Consider a three-surface
\Sigma\in\{\Sigma_{i}\}
with a boundary
C=\partial\Sigma
.
The generators or momentum maps
H[\xi]
associated to symmetry transformations
\delta_{\xi}
that are parameterized by a gauge parameter
\xi
are defined via the Hamiltonian flow equation. Given a symplectic potential
\Theta_{\Sigma}
associated with a hypersurface
\Sigma
and a corresponding symplectic form
\Omega_{\Sigma}=\bbvar{d}\Theta_{\Sigma}
, the Hamiltonian flow equation takes the form

\bbvar{I}_{\xi}\Omega_{\Sigma}=-\bbvar{d}H_{\Sigma}[\xi].

(1)

The flow equations (
1
) often pose stricter conditions on the transformation parameters than the direct application of the Noether theorem would suggest.

\displaystyle H_{\Sigma}[\xi]

\displaystyle=\bbvar{I}_{\xi}\Theta_{\Sigma}-\!\!\int_{\Sigma}\!R[\xi],

(2)

where we denote

\delta_{\xi}L=\mathrm{d}R[\xi],\qquad\delta_{\xi}S=\int_{\Sigma}R[\xi]

(3)

for Lagrangian symmetries. Since most symmetries we are going to deal with are gauge symmetries, we expect that their generators vanish on shell in the interior of
\Sigma
, but not necessarily at its boundary
C
. The corner part of a generator
H[\xi]
is called
q[\xi]
:

H_{\Sigma}[\xi]\approx q_{C}[\xi].

(4)

Here and in what follows, we denote with
\approx
equality up to equations of motion. In what follows, we drop the subscripts
\Sigma
and
C
and assume that all generators are defined at the same surface.

1
Abelian BF theory with Weyl spinors

1.1
Action, field equations and kinematical phase space

Our starting point is the following first-order complex action:

S[\psi,\phi,\pi,\rho]=\mathrm{i}\int_{M}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi_{A}\wedge\mathrm{d}\psi^{A}+\rho_{A}\wedge\mathrm{d}\phi^{A}+g\pi_{A}\wedge\rho^{A}}}\right),

(5)

where the symbol “
\mathrm{d}
” denotes the exterior derivative and
g
is a coupling constant. This action defines a topological field theory, namely, a BF theory (or 2-BF theory
28
,
33
,
56
,
13
). This can be seen by repackaging the fields to recover the usual form of a BF theory. We introduce

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}=\begin{pmatrix}\pi_{A}&\rho_{A}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathcal{A}=\begin{pmatrix}\psi^{A}\\
\phi^{A}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathcal{F}=\mathrm{d}{\cal A},\quad t(\mathcal{B})=g\begin{pmatrix}\rho^{A}\\
-\pi^{A}\end{pmatrix}.

(6)

The action
S[\psi,\phi,\pi,\rho]
is then equivalent to

\displaystyle S[\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}]=\mathrm{i}\int_{M}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\langle{\cal B}\wedge{\cal F}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle{\cal B}\wedge t({\cal B})\rangle}}\right).

(7)

The equations of motion are readily found to be

\displaystyle g\pi_{A}-\mathrm{d}\phi_{A}=0,

(8a)

\displaystyle g\rho_{A}+\mathrm{d}\psi_{A}=0,

(8b)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\pi_{A}=0,

(8c)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\rho_{A}=0,

(8d)

Note that the latter two equations (
8c
,
8d
) are merely integrability conditions: they can be obtained for any
g\neq 0
from the first pair of equations by acting with an exterior derivative on them. The special case of zero coupling will be described below. We will refer to the equations (
8a
) and (
8b
) as
fake-flatness equations
and to the second pair of equations, i.e. (
8c
) and (
8d
), as
2-flatness equations
in correspondence with the higher gauge theory terminology sometimes used in the topological field theory literature
6
,
28
,
13
.

In the variation of the action, a boundary term appears, which determines the symplectic current, from which we obtain the pre-symplectic potential
\Theta
, which is a 1-form on field space:

\Theta_{\Sigma}=\mathrm{i}\int_{\partial M}(\pi_{A}\wedge\bbvar{d}\psi^{A}+\rho_{A}\wedge\bbvar{d}\phi^{A}),

(9)

where
\bbvar{d}
denotes the field-space exterior derivative and the implicit pullback to
\Sigma
is understood. Namely, if
\varphi_{\Sigma}:\Sigma\hookrightarrow M
is the canonical embedding of the hypersurface
\Sigma
into
M
, integration over
\Sigma
selects the pullback
\varphi^{\ast}_{\Sigma}\psi_{A},\,\varphi^{\ast}_{\Sigma}\pi_{A}
, etc., of the phase-space fields. The pre-symplectic potential (
9
) immediately defines the pre-symplectic 2-form

\Omega_{\Sigma}=\mathrm{i}\int_{\Sigma}(\bbvar{d}\pi_{A}\wedge\bbvar{d}\psi^{A}+\bbvar{d}\rho_{A}\wedge\bbvar{d}\phi^{A}).

(10)

1.2
Symmetries, generators and their algebra

In this section, we analyse the symmetries of the topological theory, using the covariant phase space formalism. We will recover the standard topological symmetries of the BF-type of action (
5
), specified by translations of the 1-connection
\mathcal{A}
and exact shift symmetries of the
\mathcal{B}
-field, as introduced in (
6
) above. Diffeomorphisms can be recovered from the latter as a combination of shift transformations with field-dependent gauge parameters. Unlike the usual BF-theories, we will identify some additional hidden symmetries. There is a global
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
symmetry, as well as a
dual

\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
symmetry. To avoid confusion with the global symmetry, we denote it by
\overline{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})}
. This symmetry will be obtained by considering a certain class of field-dependent shift symmetries.

Shift symmetry

Let
\eta_{A},\zeta_{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\Omega^{1}(M)
be Weyl spinor–valued 1-forms which will parameterize the shift transformations. The action (
5
) is invariant under two sets of shift transformations:

\displaystyle\delta_{\eta}\psi_{A}

\displaystyle=g\,\eta_{A},

\displaystyle\delta_{\eta}\phi_{A}

\displaystyle=0,

(11a)

\displaystyle\delta_{\eta}\rho_{A}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{d}\eta_{A},

\displaystyle\delta_{\eta}\pi_{A}

\displaystyle=0,

(11b)

and

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta}\phi_{A}

\displaystyle=g\,\zeta_{A},

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta}\psi_{A}

\displaystyle=0,

(12a)

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta}\pi_{A}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\zeta_{A},

\displaystyle\delta_{\zeta}\rho_{A}

\displaystyle=0.

(12b)

If the gauge parameters parameters
\eta
and
\zeta
are field-independent, the corresponding generators are integrable and given by

\displaystyle\bbvar{I}_{\eta}\Omega

\displaystyle=-\bbvar{d}H_{\phi}[\eta],

\displaystyle H_{\phi}[\eta]

\displaystyle=F_{\phi}[\eta]+q_{\phi}[\eta],

(13a)

\displaystyle\bbvar{I}_{\zeta}\Omega

\displaystyle=-\bbvar{d}H_{\psi}[\zeta],

\displaystyle H_{\psi}[\zeta]

\displaystyle=F_{\psi}[\zeta]+q_{\psi}[\zeta],

(13b)

where the momentum maps correspond to the equations of motion (
8a
)–(
8b
),

\displaystyle F_{\phi}[\eta]=\mathrm{i}\int_{\Sigma}(g\pi_{A}-\mathrm{d}\phi_{A})\wedge\eta^{A},

(14a)

\displaystyle F_{\psi}[\zeta]=\mathrm{i}\int_{\Sigma}(g\rho_{A}+\mathrm{d}\psi_{A})\wedge\zeta^{A}

(14b)

(mnemonic:
F
stands for
fake-flatness
), and corner charges are given by

\displaystyle q_{\phi}[\eta]=\mathrm{i}\int_{C}\eta_{A}\wedge\phi^{A},

(15a)

\displaystyle q_{\psi}[\zeta]=\mathrm{i}\int_{C}\zeta^{A}\wedge\psi_{A}.

(15b)

Exact shift symmetries

Let us focus on the subspace of exact shift symmetries. It is easy to notice that
\mathrm{d}
-exact shift transformations leave the momenta invariant.

Let
\lambda_{A},\mu_{A}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\otimes\Omega^{0}(M)
be Weyl spinor fields. The two sets of the
\mathrm{d}
-exact shifts have the following action:

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}\psi_{A}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\lambda_{A},

\displaystyle\delta_{\lambda}\phi_{A}=\delta_{\lambda}\pi_{A}=\delta_{\lambda}\rho_{A}=0,

(16a)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mu}\phi_{A}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\mu_{A},

\displaystyle\delta_{\mu}\psi_{A}=\delta_{\mu}\pi_{A}=\delta_{\mu}\rho_{A}=0.

(16b)

The Hamiltonian flow equation now takes the form:

\displaystyle\bbvar{I}_{\lambda}\Omega

\displaystyle\equiv-\bbvar{d}H_{\pi}[\lambda],

\displaystyle H_{\pi}[\lambda]

\displaystyle=E_{\pi}[\lambda]+q_{\pi}[\lambda],

(17a)

\displaystyle\bbvar{I}_{\mu}\Omega

\displaystyle\equiv-\bbvar{d}H_{\rho}[\mu],

\displaystyle H_{\rho}[\mu]

\displaystyle=E_{\rho}[\mu]+q_{\rho}[\mu],

(17b)

where the momentum maps correspond to the remaining equations of motion (
8c
)–(
8d
),

\displaystyle E_{\pi}[\lambda]=\mathrm{i}\int_{\Sigma}\mathrm{d}\pi^{A}\lambda_{A},

(18a)

\displaystyle E_{\rho}[\mu]=\mathrm{i}\int_{\Sigma}\mathrm{d}\rho^{A}\mu_{A}.

(18b)

(mnemonic:
E
stands for
Einstein equations
in correspondence with their role in the gravitational sector), and corner charges are, respectively:

\displaystyle q_{\pi}[\lambda]=\mathrm{i}\int_{C}\pi_{A}\lambda^{A},

(19a)

\displaystyle q_{\phi}[\mu]=\mathrm{i}\int_{C}\rho_{A}\mu^{A}.

(19b)

We note that the on-shell charges for the shift and the exact shifts are related:

\displaystyle q_{\pi}[\lambda]=\mathrm{i}\int_{C}\pi_{A}\lambda^{A}\overset{\partial C=\emptyset}{\approx}\frac{\mathrm{i}}{g}\int_{C}\mathrm{d}\lambda_{A}\wedge\phi^{A}=q_{\phi}[g^{-1}\mathrm{d}\lambda],

(20a)

\displaystyle q_{\rho}[\mu]=\mathrm{i}\int_{C}\rho_{A}\mu^{A}\overset{\partial C=\emptyset}{\approx}\frac{\mathrm{i}}{g}\int_{C}\mathrm{d}\mu^{A}\wedge\psi_{A}=q_{\psi}[g^{-1}\mathrm{d}\mu].

(20b)

The fact that
\mathrm{d}
-exact shifts generated by the 2-flatness constraint are a subset of shifts generated by the fake-flatness conditions is consistent with the redundancy of the system of constraints: in the abelian theory, the 2-flatness equations (
8c
)–(
8d
) follow from fake-flatness (
8a
)–(
8b
).

Higher-gauge theories
6
,
13
are normally symmetric under two tiers of gauge transformations satisfying certain coherence relations. Exact shifts (
16
) and shifts (
11
)–(
12
) represent this structure in disguise. The division between the 1-gauge (exact shifts) and 2-gauge (full shifts) has been washed away due to
the gauge algebra being abelian, but will be restored in the
g\rightarrow 0
(skeletal) limit, see
Section
4
.

Diffeomorphisms

The next gauge symmetry to be considered here is the four-dimensional diffeomorphism symmetry. They are generated on phase-space by the Lie derivative,
\delta^{di\!f\!\!f}_{\xi}=L_{\xi}\equiv\mathrm{d}\iota_{\xi}+\iota_{\xi}\mathrm{d}
. This is where the difference between the definitions of the generator in terms of the Hamiltonian flow equation (
1
) or from Noether theorem (
2
) is manifest. The Noether charge can be expressed according to (
2
) for an arbitrary vector field
\xi\in\Gamma(TM)
in terms of the momentum maps introduced above and a corner charge as

\displaystyle H^{di\!f\!\!f}[\xi]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{Noether1}}{=}F_{\psi}[\iota_{\xi}\pi]-F_{\phi}[\iota_{\xi}\rho]+E_{\pi}[\iota_{\xi}\psi]+E_{\rho}[\iota_{\xi}\phi]+q^{di\!f\!\!f}[\xi],

(21a)

\displaystyle q^{di\!f\!\!f}[\xi]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\int_{C}(\iota_{\xi}\psi^{A}\pi_{A}+\iota_{\xi}\phi^{A}\rho_{A}).

(21b)

Hence we have recovered here a standard result of BF-theory: On shell, the diffeomorphism charges can be built from the shift symmetries with field dependent parameters.

Note that in the special case where the vector field
\xi\in\Gamma(TM)
is tangential to the corner, i.e.
\xi|_{C}\in T{C}
, we obtain further that

\displaystyle q^{di\!f\!\!f}[\xi]

\displaystyle\equiv q_{\pi}[\iota_{\xi}\psi]+q_{\rho}[\iota_{\xi}\phi]\overset{\xi|_{C}\in T{C}}{=}-q_{\phi}[\iota_{\xi}\rho]+q_{\psi}[\iota_{\xi}\pi]\equiv

(22a)

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(-q_{\phi}[\iota_{\xi}\rho]+q_{\psi}[\iota_{\xi}\pi]+q_{\pi}[\iota_{\xi}\psi]+q_{\rho}[\iota_{\xi}\phi]}}\right).

(22b)

This in turn implies that the corner contribution of shift generators corresponds to one-half of the bulk generators. The full field-dependent shift generators will be recovered upon addition of the anti-self-dual sector, which we will discuss in
Section
3
. In fact, the identity (
22a
) holds due to the following two observations:

\displaystyle q_{\phi}[\iota_{\xi}\rho]+q_{\rho}[\iota_{\xi}\phi]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\int_{C}\iota_{\xi}(\rho_{A}\wedge\phi^{A})\overset{\xi|_{C}\in T{C}}{=}0,

(23a)

\displaystyle-q_{\psi}[\iota_{\xi}\pi]+q_{\pi}[\iota_{\xi}\psi]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\int_{C}\iota_{\xi}(\pi_{A}\wedge\psi^{A})\overset{\xi|_{C}\in T{C}}{=}0.

(23b)

Hamilton’s flow equation for diffeomorphisms is, however, integrable on
\Sigma
only if
\xi|_{\Sigma}\in T\Sigma
and
\xi|_{C}\in T{C}
. It then takes the form

\bbvar{I}_{\xi}\Omega=-\bbvar{d}H^{di\!f\!\!f}[\xi].

(24)

On-shell, the tangency of
\xi
to
\Sigma
is relaxed and the Hamilton’s flow equation is integrable provided the gauge parameter is tangent to the corner, i.e.
\xi|_{C}\in TC
.

\overline{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})}
local symmetry

There are yet another kind of field-dependent shift transformations which turn out to be integrable and play a crucial role in the gravitational theory. These transformations are parametrized by three complex fields
\{a,b,c\}\equiv\beta
on spacetime. They act on the phase space as rotations in the spinor plane, as

\displaystyle\delta_{\beta}\psi_{A}=a\psi_{A}+b\phi_{A},

(25a)

\displaystyle\delta_{\beta}\phi_{A}=c\psi_{A}-a\phi_{A},

(25b)

\displaystyle\delta_{\beta}\pi_{A}=-c\rho_{A}-a\pi_{A}+\frac{1}{g}\mathrm{d}c\wedge\psi_{A}-\frac{1}{g}\mathrm{d}a\wedge\phi_{A},

(25c)

\displaystyle\delta_{\beta}\rho_{A}=a\rho_{A}-b\pi_{A}-\frac{1}{g}\mathrm{d}a\wedge\psi_{A}-\frac{1}{g}\mathrm{d}b\wedge\phi_{A}.

(25d)

The corresponding off-shell generator is

H^{\overline{\mathfrak{sl}}}[\beta]=\frac{1}{g}F_{\phi}[a\psi+b\phi]+\frac{1}{g}F_{\psi}[c\psi-a\phi]+q^{\overline{\mathfrak{sl}}}[\beta],

(26)

where the corner charge is given by

\displaystyle q^{\overline{\mathfrak{sl}}}[\beta]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{g}\int_{C}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{c}{2}\,\psi^{A}\wedge\psi_{A}-\frac{b}{2}\,\phi^{A}\wedge\phi_{A}-a\,\psi^{A}\wedge\phi_{A}}}\right)=

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2g}\int_{C}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\psi^{A}\wedge\delta_{\beta}\phi_{A}-\delta_{\beta}\psi^{A}\wedge\phi_{A}}}\right)=

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2g}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{\psi}[\delta_{\beta}\phi]+q_{\phi}[\delta_{\beta}\psi]}}\right).

(27)

While not obvious from the action (
5
), this symmetry can be guessed from the connection of this model to gravity. It becomes manifest that these transformations generate a dual
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
symmetry, when considering its Poisson algebra below in
Section
2
.

\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
global symmetry

The action (
5
) is also invariant under a
global

\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
symmetry. If
\alpha\in\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})
, the infinitesimal
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
action is given by

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}\psi_{A}=\alpha_{A}{}^{B}\psi_{B},

(28a)

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}\phi_{A}=\alpha_{A}{}^{B}\phi_{B},

(28b)

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}\pi_{A}=\alpha_{A}{}^{B}\pi_{B},

(28c)

\displaystyle\delta_{\alpha}\rho_{A}=\alpha_{A}{}^{B}\rho_{B}.

(28d)

Global symmetries are not typically associated with corner charges. Here, although the symmetry is global, its generator is localized at the corner on shell:

H^{\mathfrak{sl}}[\alpha]=\mathrm{i}\alpha^{AB}\int_{\Sigma}(\pi_{A}\wedge\psi_{B}+\rho_{A}\wedge\phi_{B})\approx-\mathrm{i}g^{-1}\alpha^{AB}\int_{C}\psi_{(A}\wedge\phi_{B)}\equiv q^{\mathfrak{sl}}[\alpha].

(29)

We will also show that the Poisson algebra of such charges is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})
.

Poisson algebra

In what follows, we determine the Poisson algebra of the different momentum maps using the identity

\{H_{\xi_{1}},H_{\xi_{2}}\}=\bbvar{I}_{\xi_{2}}\bbvar{I}_{\xi_{1}}\Omega.

(30)

The generators associated to the shift and the exact shifts (
13
,
17
) Poisson commute off-shell up to central extensions

\displaystyle\{H_{\phi}[\eta],H_{\rho}[\mu]\}=\mathrm{i}\int_{C}\mu_{A}\mathrm{d}\eta^{A},

(31a)

\displaystyle\{H_{\psi}[\zeta],H_{\pi}[\lambda]\}=\mathrm{i}\int_{C}\lambda^{A}\mathrm{d}\zeta_{A},

(31b)

\displaystyle\{H_{\phi}[\eta],H_{\psi}[\zeta]\}=\mathrm{i}\,g\int_{C}\eta^{A}\wedge\zeta_{A}.

(31c)

Considering now the Poisson algebra associated with the diffeomorphisms generated by
\xi,\chi|_{\Sigma}\in T\Sigma
and
\xi,\chi|_{C}\in T{C}
, we recover the expected algebra,

\{H^{di\!f\!\!f}[\chi],H^{di\!f\!\!f}[\xi]\}=H^{di\!f\!\!f}\big[[\xi,\chi]\big],

(32)

where
[\cdot,\cdot]
is the Lie bracket of vector fields. We can also determine the Poisson algebra mixing the different sectors, namely the shift and d-exact shift symmetries and diffeomorphisms

\displaystyle\{H_{\phi}[\eta],H^{di\!f\!\!f}[\xi]\}

\displaystyle=H_{\phi}[L_{\xi}\eta],

\displaystyle\{H_{\pi}[\lambda],H^{di\!f\!\!f}[\xi]\}=H_{\pi}[L_{\xi}\lambda],

(33a)

\displaystyle\{H_{\psi}[\zeta],H^{di\!f\!\!f}[\xi]\}

\displaystyle=H_{\psi}[L_{\xi}\zeta]

\displaystyle\{H_{\rho}[\mu],H^{di\!f\!\!f}[\xi]\}=H_{\rho}[L_{\xi}\mu].

(33b)

We have recovered, in this way, the standard Poisson algebra of the topological theory.

A direct calculation shows that the Poisson algebra of the field-dependent shift generators (
26
) associated with what we called
\overline{\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})}
, is indeed the
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})
algebra:

\{H^{\overline{\mathfrak{sl}}}[\beta_{1}],H^{\overline{\mathfrak{sl}}}[\beta_{2}]\}=-H^{\overline{\mathfrak{sl}}}\big[[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}]\big]

(34)

with its parameters identified with traceless matrices as

\{\beta_{A^{\prime}}{}^{B^{\prime}}\}=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&-a\end{pmatrix},

(35)

and

\mathchoice{[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B^{\prime}}}}_{{{A^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B^{\prime}}}}_{{{A^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B^{\prime}}}}_{{{A^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{[\beta_{1},\beta_{2}]^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B^{\prime}}}}_{{{A^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.23048pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.42502pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29286pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}=\mathchoice{\beta_{1}{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.8614pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.1375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0875pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{C^{\prime}}}}_{{{A^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[8.07347pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[8.07347pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.3151pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4. ptstyle$}}}}}

(36)

is the
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})
Lie bracket.

The global
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
generators (
29
) form a closed Poisson algebra too,

\{H^{\mathfrak{sl}}[\alpha_{1}],H^{\mathfrak{sl}}[\alpha_{2}]\}=-H^{\mathfrak{sl}}\big[[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}]\big].

(37)

Let
(\alpha\eta)_{A}\equiv\alpha_{A}{}^{B}\eta_{B}
denote an action of an
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})
algebra element
\alpha
on a spinor
\eta
. If
\xi|_{\Sigma}\in T\Sigma
and
\xi|_{C}\in T{C}
,

\displaystyle\{H_{\phi}[\eta],H^{\mathfrak{sl}}[\alpha]\}=H_{\phi}[\alpha\eta],\qquad\{H_{\pi}[\lambda],H^{\mathfrak{sl}}[\alpha]\}=H_{\pi}[\alpha\lambda],

(38a)

\displaystyle\{H_{\psi}[\zeta],H^{\mathfrak{sl}}[\alpha]\}=H_{\psi}[\alpha\zeta],\qquad\{H_{\rho}[\mu],H^{\mathfrak{sl}}[\alpha]\}=H_{\rho}[\alpha\mu],

(38b)

\displaystyle\{H^{di\!f\!\!f}[\xi],H^{\mathfrak{sl}}[\alpha]\}=0.

(38c)

Thus, while the
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
symmetry of the action (
5
) is global, it behaves like a generator of a gauge transformation. We are going to use this insight to promote the global symmetry into a local gauge symmetry, thereby transforming the topological theory into self-dual gravity. Throughout this section, we assumed
g\neq 0
. For the study of the
g=0
case, see
Section
4.1
below.

2
Gravity and self-dual gravity from the topological theory

2.1
Action

In this section, we are going to show why the topological theory (
5
) is our starting point for studying gravity. Namely, we will argue that gravity can be formulated as this topological theory with a local
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
symmetry imposed. To do so, we demand that the
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
bulk generator
(
29
)
vanishes on-shell by adding a Lagrange-multiplier term to the action (
5
). The Lagrange multiplier 1-form
A_{AB}
can be interpreted as a self-dual
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
connection, which renders the action locally
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
-invariant via the introduction of covariant derivatives:

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\int_{M}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi_{A}\wedge\mathrm{d}\psi^{A}+\rho_{A}\wedge\mathrm{d}\phi^{A}+g\pi_{A}\wedge\rho^{A}+A^{AB}\wedge(\pi_{A}\wedge\psi_{B}+\rho_{A}\wedge\phi_{B})}}\right)\equiv

(39a)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathrm{i}\int_{M}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi_{A}\wedge\nabla\psi^{A}+\rho_{A}\wedge\nabla\phi^{A}+g\pi_{A}\wedge\rho^{A}}}\right),

(39b)

where
\nabla=\mathrm{d}+A
acts on spinor-valued
p
-forms as

\nabla\psi_{A}=\mathrm{d}\psi_{A}+A_{A}^{\,\,\,\,B}\wedge\psi_{B}.

(40)

The action (
39b
) is the same as the one proposed by Robinson in
39
up to the relative coupling
g
in the last term, which we will explain to be related to Newton’s constant.

It is also useful to define the curvature of such a connection
1
1

1

Note that we are using the convention of
14
,
5
.

F_{AB}=\mathrm{d}A_{AB}+A_{A}{}^{C}\wedge A_{CB}.

(41)

Note that while both
A_{A}{}^{B}
and
F_{A}{}^{B}
are
\mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{C})
-valued, their all-upper- and all-lower-index counterparts are symmetric matrices obtained through a contraction with the
\epsilon
-tensor:

A_{AB}=A_{A}{}^{C}\epsilon_{CB}=A_{(AB)},\qquad A^{AB}=\epsilon^{AC}A_{C}{}^{B}=A^{(AB)},\quad\text{etc.}

(42)

This property is extensively used in the calculations below.

Let us now step back for a moment and show the relationship between this action and gravity. Let the capital Latin letters from the second half of the alphabet
I,J,...
label internal Lorentz four-vector components and take values in
\{0,...,3\}
. Following the conventions from
30
, we use an anti-hermitian basis
2
2

2

Here,
\sigma^{i}_{AA^{\prime}}
corresponds to an
i
-th Pauli matrix and
\sigma^{0}_{AA^{\prime}}=\mathbf{1}_{AA^{\prime}}
is the identity. Objects with a different placement of indices can be obtained from these via multiplication by an
\epsilon
-tensor and contraction with internal Minkowski metric
\eta_{IJ}
. The choice of an anti-hermitian basis corresponds to the
(-,+,+,+)
metric signature.

\{\mathrm{i}\sigma^{I}_{AA^{\prime}}\}
to convert internal four-vectors into
2\times 2
complex matrices via

x_{AA^{\prime}}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{2}}\sigma_{AA^{\prime}}{}^{I}x_{I}.

(43)

An image of such a map is an anti-hermitian matrix
if and only if
the vector
x^{I}
is real. The inverse map is, similarly,

x^{I}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{2}}\sigma_{AA^{\prime}}{}^{I}x^{AA^{\prime}}.

(44)

Using this correspondence, we replace a tetrad 1-form
\mathchoice{e^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{e^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{e^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{e^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}

A_{A}{}^{B}

\omega_{AA^{\prime}BB^{\prime}}=-\frac{1}{2}\mathchoice{\sigma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.87532pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.87532pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.8125pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.7125pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{I}}}_{{{AA^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\sigma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.87532pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.87532pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.8125pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.7125pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{I}}}_{{{AA^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\sigma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.87532pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.87532pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.8125pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.7125pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{I}}}_{{{AA^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\sigma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.87532pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.87532pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.8125pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.7125pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{I}}}_{{{AA^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathchoice{\sigma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[14.61348pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.61348pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.38754pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.12323pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}_{{{BB^{\prime}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\s
